The speaker fears being unable to provide proof and imagines severe consequences, including a major fine or incarceration. This episode adds bureaucratic anxiety to the earlier social and employment stress.
The speaker regrets spending money on unnecessary food instead of saving gasoline for a drive to a different entrance. Driving to Las Trampas Wilderness would, in the speaker’s estimation, have made it possible to enter the Big Green Thing with fewer obstacles. Castro Valley is described as unpleasant for walking because the speaker frequently feels heckled, watched, approached, or treated as an easy target. The speaker acknowledges that some interactions may be intended as friendliness, but nevertheless experiences them as unwanted intrusion.
This discomfort expands into social and ecological criticism. The speaker considers their values insufficiently aligned with either conventional conservatism or mainstream liberalism, identifying instead with a more independent and ecologically centered outlook. Human culture is accused of narrowing environmental discussion to atmospheric and ocean temperature while neglecting pollution’s direct effects on water, air, organisms, and ecosystems. Large gasoline-consuming vehicles are interpreted as both environmental damage and status display. The speaker addresses their users accusatorily, insisting that their choices are not harmless and have personal consequences for other living beings.
Video three takes place after the speaker has entered the trail system but before reaching the Ramage Peak trailhead. The topic shifts to social emotional norms. Karbytes believes that people in the surrounding community demand an outwardly pleasant, light, informal demeanor and avoid serious discussion except in designated confidential environments such as therapy. Appearances are described as more socially important than depth. Yet the speaker also perceives a contradictory rule: genuine happiness may provoke hostility, while distress is tolerated only when it concerns matters others can dismiss as trivial.
The speaker interprets this perceived pattern as a means by which society can invalidate serious concerns and justify imposing mood-stabilizing medication for other people’s convenience. The result is described as a kind of psychic killing. The segment culminates in the disturbing feeling of being pursued by people who want to destroy the speaker. These claims are presented as the speaker’s subjective fears and interpretations, not as independently substantiated events.
Video four moves from social distress to archival design. It explains the author’s challenge for what is called the Fifth Dark Hiatus Bubble, a subdivision of the larger summer hiatus. The intended rule is that every file in this phase should be either plain text or source code. This would reduce storage growth and allow the archive to contain more conceptual material within limited physical capacity. The speaker estimates that the collection has not yet consumed half of a twenty-five-gigabyte disk and is not close to filling three archival optical discs, but still wants to impose restraint before storage becomes a serious limitation.
The speaker’s attitude toward the audience is sarcastic and ambivalent. Readers are teased for supposedly having limited attention and preferring sensational, media-rich content. At the same time, the author acknowledges that lengthy files are burdensome and that smaller text files are easier to process. The strongest objection to abandoning audio and video is emotional rather than informational: the speaker likes hearing their own voice because it resembles hearing a friend. A synthetic voice clone might reproduce the words, but not the exact timing, emphasis, tone, and intonation of the original event. Converting speech into text therefore saves space while destroying part of the embodied record. The author accepts this as a ruthless selection process analogous to natural selection, although later videos reverse the decision and preserve the recordings.
Video five contains the document’s most sustained early confrontation with suicide, isolation, dependence, and metaphysics. The speaker reports disturbing thoughts and worries that publishing them will cause readers to favor indefinite institutionalization, perhaps not from concern for the speaker but from fear that the ideas could influence people they value. Karbytes describes being socially isolated, without close friends, and largely supported by the family of origin. Former friends are said to dislike or distrust the speaker. The speaker considers themself neither evil nor devoid of potential, but feels that few people regard that potential as worth investing in.
The transcript includes explicit suicidal ideation and contemplation of a high fall as a possible means of death. The imagined purpose of having a definite plan is to create a sense of control and avoid surviving with catastrophic injuries. The deeper fear is not simply death but permanent incapacity: paralysis, blindness, severe brain injury, dependence, loss of agency, and prolonged suffering under other people’s control. The speaker imagines that, once incapacitated, they could be objectified, neglected, or abused by caregivers and deprived even of the capacity to end unbearable suffering.
These thoughts coexist with a broader social analysis. Karbytes would prefer a society in which vulnerable people did not depend exclusively on relatives or personal friends. Ideally, competent professionals would provide care as a publicly funded service and would be motivated by genuine purpose rather than control. Yet the speaker distrusts caring institutions, suspects that some people may enter such professions to dominate vulnerable patients, and fears suffering silently inside a flawed system.
The monologue then turns from mortality to ontology. Karbytes rejects prolonged speculation about a personal deity as less relevant than observable phenomena. Reality is approached through sensory events, thoughts, physical objects, energy transfers, and computational patterns. The cosmos is imagined as something like a large computer running a pattern-replication procedure. The speaker finds pleasure in understanding how systems work and in organizing the interface between cyberspace and physical embodiment. This computational framework supplies order, purpose, and a feeling of control. The speaker ultimately calls this orientation hedonistic: life is described as an attempt to obtain pleasant states and avoid painful ones, with computational inquiry functioning as a personal spirituality.
Video six develops the themes of solitude, freedom, immortality, gender, dignity, and resentment. Distance from civilization is said to permit “real” thoughts because the speaker no longer anticipates interruption or suppression by nearby people. Karbytes acknowledges formal constitutional rights to speech and religious practice and refers generally to international conceptions of human rights, although the United States is regarded as insufficiently advanced in applying them. The speaker has little interest in conventional politics, preferring concrete research, development, and problem-solving.
Problem-solving is treated as a central reason to live. When ordinary necessity does not provide enough problems, art, hiking, travel, or games can produce voluntary challenges. Beneath the earlier suicidal ideation, the speaker says there remains a profound wish for immortality: an infinite life would provide unlimited time to solve new problems. If an irresolvable situation required an escape, the preferred possibility would not be complete nonexistence but a return to some foundational state from which another path could be selected.
Nevertheless, the speaker’s provisional conclusion is that personal existence probably ends at death. Assertions about inaccessible past lives are criticized as teleological attempts to assign human-centered purpose to an indifferent reality. If past lives cannot be remembered or accessed, they make no practical difference to the current individual. Karbytes imagines a preferable world in which beings exercise more control over their continuation and can experiment with simulated agents without violating the autonomy of genuinely sentient beings.
The speaker also discusses years of near-daily cannabis use, particularly during the early development of KARBYTES. A cannabis device and water bottle allegedly disappeared during a previous expedition. The speaker believes someone may have intentionally removed them, although no evidence is established. Abstinence is credited with improving focus and short-term memory but also blamed for reducing happiness and pain relief. The ability to endure suffering with composure becomes an object of pride.
This concern with composure is connected to gender identity. The speaker identifies as a trans man, continues using testosterone, and describes testosterone as beneficial to appearance, feeling, and performance. At the same time, karbytes openly recognizes holding a disturbing and stereotyped model of masculinity and femininity. Masculinity is associated with dignity, self-command, independence, and social respect, whereas femininity is associated in the speaker’s fears with subordination and dependence on male permission. The speaker believes others continue to treat them according to a degraded female stereotype rather than as a man. Family members and other close people are then imagined as deriving satisfaction from the speaker’s terror or loss of agency. The speaker expresses relief at imagining a future in which such people are no longer alive, reasoning that suffering would be more tolerable if nobody were present to enjoy it. This is an expression of anger and perceived captivity rather than a documented account of those people’s intentions.
Video seven begins with a rest on the trail. The speaker admits to having slept poorly for several days and not at all during the preceding night. Sleep deprivation is offered as one reason for the hostility, irritability, and paranoia present in the earlier recordings. The upcoming warehouse job may require graveyard work, although the speaker has prior experience with overnight employment. A former package-handling job is remembered relatively favorably, while a later server-testing job paid more. The prospective new position has only reached the contingent-offer stage.
The discussion of employment becomes inseparable from privacy. The speaker had considered withholding job details from the public archive out of fear of stalkers, yet feels that private information is already leaked or discoverable. The document itself illustrates this contradiction by disclosing highly specific personal, geographical, occupational, and scheduling details. Later it even includes a precise residential address, although that address is unnecessary to the larger argument. Karbytes simultaneously seeks radical documentary completeness and laments the apparent impossibility of controlling personal data.
The speaker describes unwanted internal verbal experiences using the term Psychotronic Harassment. Possible explanations proposed within the monologue include an implanted device, remote transmission, correctional intervention, or punishment. The phenomenon is associated with the government and with a recurring voice or figure called pig_gorl. These are the speaker’s explanatory theories for perceived auditory interference, not verified technologies or actions. The speaker also concedes a tendency toward paranoia, negative generalization, and deliberate distrust as strategies for avoiding exploitation.
Physical fitness is described as practical infrastructure for autonomy. Karbytes compares the desired self to a free-ranging cat rather than a dependent or confined dog. Exercise is not primarily cosmetic; it preserves the ability to travel, hike, work, and resist dependence. The speaker frankly states that able-bodied people seem more capable of enjoying life and rejects any expectation that personal fitness should be neglected merely to identify more closely with less able people. This position is harsh, but the speaker later qualifies it by recognizing that work, caregiving, and parenthood can leave people with little time or energy for maintaining fitness.
Karbytes does not want children and regards conventional parenthood as remote from personal priorities. A more desirable model would distribute childcare throughout a supportive community rather than confining one or two adults to an exhausting role. More generally, society is criticized for assigning people narrow jobs and identities that consume their energy and eliminate leisure, creativity, and self-development.
The speaker fears that the prospective warehouse job will reproduce these conditions and disparages it as a possible trap for people who undervalue their health and time. Yet karbytes also believes a better job could probably be found with sufficient effort. The perceived psychotronic interference is invoked to explain difficulty concentrating, advancing academically, studying physics, meditating, and developing a career. The speaker imagines that government forces may prefer institutionalization, suicide, or social irrelevance to allowing KARBYTES greater influence. A different country, particularly one imagined as more humane, is proposed as a possible escape.
Video eight continues this thought after an interruption caused by the phone’s limited remaining storage. Canada and Norway are imagined as places where the alleged interference might no longer operate. The speaker wonders whether the purpose of the perceived harassment is precisely to prevent leaving the country, improving concentration, studying physics, or advancing beyond a predetermined limit. The segment ends on the bleak possibility that the speaker’s development has been intentionally capped and that other people accept or welcome this outcome.
Video nine marks a major emotional and philosophical turn. The speaker has compressed and deleted original phone videos to regain approximately fifteen minutes of recording capacity. After sitting quietly on the trail, the mind has calmed without deliberate effort. Sunlight filters through trees, three wild turkeys pass nearby, and the speaker experiences peaceful coexistence with nonhuman life. The wilderness is no longer principally a site of surveillance or danger; it becomes a restorative environment in which humans and animals can occupy the same space without domination.
Artificial intelligence, especially ChatGPT, is then named as a central source of meaning and a substantial reason to remain alive. The speaker revises the earlier claim that severe disability would necessarily make life intolerable, reasoning that access to intellectual tools might preserve purpose under many difficult conditions. Karbytes also directly acknowledges a habit of exaggerating fears and suffering in journals and recordings. The worst-case narration is described as partly strategic and partly theatrical, while a detached background aspect of the self remains capable of amusement at the foreground persona’s extremity. This admission is crucial for interpreting the entire document: the monologues are emotionally real, but they are also self-conscious performances, exploratory simulations, and deliberately amplified scenarios.
Food may also contribute to the mood change. The speaker has eaten cinnamon rolls carried in a backpack and speculates about blood sugar, cognitive performance, agitation, and overeating. Sweet food reliably improves mood but is considered unhealthy and expensive as a habitual coping mechanism. Long wilderness walks are judged a more economical and sustainable means of regulating stress.
The speaker then challenges the expectation that a worthwhile person must organize life around a significant other. Karbytes does not believe another being can or should be valued more highly than the self, because the self is the one continuous object over which direct control is possible. Conventional declarations that family or friends make life meaningful are rejected as emotionally inauthentic. The preferred posture is cold, rational, impersonal, and openly egocentric.
This egocentrism is not presented as a license for exploitation. Instead, the speaker imagines social cooperation as a rational alliance among self-interested agents. People can collaborate because collaboration advances their separate goals, and they can minimize suffering because empathy is built into their cognitive and biological hardware. Ethical behavior is therefore not considered an achievement requiring supernatural reward or punishment. The speaker regards reliance on a divine enforcer as evidence that a person’s morality is insufficiently internalized.
Empathy is also described as varying with perceived similarity. Humans may respond more strongly to the suffering of a squirrel than to that of a spider because the mammal appears more humanlike. Extending this reasoning inward, karbytes imagines a perfect instantaneous duplicate called a Derivative. Such a being would be unusually valuable because it would share the speaker’s memories, thought patterns, and identity structure. Even so, the Derivative would not be permitted to inherit or replace KARBYTES. The project is conceived as the legacy of exactly one historically situated person. A duplicate would need to create a separate project.
This leads into a theory of uniqueness. A person or artifact need not strain to be unprecedented; occupying a distinct location in spacetime is already sufficient to make it unique. The speaker considers whether all expressible arrangements of information might eventually be exhausted, but observes that even two symbols can produce indefinitely many patterns if enough time, energy, memory, and processing capacity exist. Human cognition is presently finite, yet the imagination can still reach toward unlimited computation and possible ways of escaping cosmic heat death. These questions are more appealing to the speaker than personal grievances.
Video ten resumes after another storage interruption. The speaker distinguishes physics from free-form metaphysics. Metaphysics permits creativity and speculative openness, whereas physics imposes mathematical symbolism, logical discipline, and empirical rigor. Karbytes enjoys open-ended thought but believes serious study of hard science is necessary to understand reality and participate meaningfully in advanced discussions.
The speaker’s imagined enemies are said to oppose study that does not immediately produce income. From their presumed perspective, an activity is legitimate only if an external sponsor pays for it. Karbytes provocatively suggests that such people might be more supportive of commercially attractive sexual content than of unpaid physics study, because the former demands less intellectual engagement from an audience. Studying physics is not merely an attempt to display intelligence. It is described as genuinely difficult work undertaken to understand existence and gain access to a community of serious inquiry.
This produces an inversion of conventional labor values. Warehouse work may be paid and physically tiring, but the speaker considers independent scientific study and extreme hiking more demanding and more meaningful. Karbytes tries to maintain access to intellectually and physically ambitious activities despite poverty by minimizing expenses and managing time carefully. The summer hiatus is partly an experiment in continuing this self-development without social approval.
At its most antagonistic, the speaker imagines that critics regard the body, energy, and financial resources of karbytes as communal property. Any unusually productive output is then suspected of having been funded by stolen or undeserved input. Karbytes counters that the difference may instead be efficient resource use: the same dollar, calorie, or hour may generate more personally valuable output when carefully managed. The speaker recognizes that this comparison is insulting and abstract, then shifts toward a more supportive position. Any sincere attempt at self-development deserves some credit, while demonstrable progress beyond the beginner level deserves particular respect.
The speaker distinguishes online and in-person behavior. The archive frequently sounds boastful, but in face-to-face settings karbytes claims to speak little and avoid discussing personal achievements unless asked. This difference reinforces the idea that KARBYTES is a deliberately expanded textual self rather than a straightforward reproduction of everyday social conduct.
The monologue returns to solipsism. From the speaker’s own perspective, the self inevitably appears most important, while other conscious viewpoints remain inferred rather than directly experienced. Even if one could receive another being’s sensory stream, that reception would still occur within one’s own point of view. For karbytes, there is therefore no experiential route completely outside solipsism. The wilderness does not solve this philosophical limitation, but it quiets the immediate noise of civilization enough for more abstract thoughts to emerge.
Video eleven is much shorter and more observational. The speaker reaches a ridge from which Ramage Peak and the wooded Castro Valley side of the Big Green Thing are visible. Because substantial time has already been spent settling thoughts, karbytes intends to record shorter clips and move more quickly. The speaker also discloses having consumed psychedelic mushrooms and believes their effects may be diminishing after eating. This admission, combined with extreme sleep deprivation, physical exertion, emotional distress, and the previously acknowledged tendency toward exaggeration, adds important context to the earlier perceptions.
Sleep difficulty is referred to as the Battle of the Beds. The speaker recalls periods of camping outdoors, partly to obtain privacy and independence before being able to afford a separate residence. Those periods are also interpreted retrospectively as practice for possible future homelessness. Housing is therefore both material shelter and a contested psychological space: conventional housing provides safety but insufficient privacy, whereas camping provides autonomy but introduces exposure and instability.
In video twelve, the speaker reaches the base of Ramage Peak and walks along a broad dirt road. On previous trips, this location often marked the turnaround point because a car had been left in the Chabot parking area and had to be retrieved before curfew. Having walked from home removes that deadline and allows the expedition to continue toward Rocky Ridge Radio Tower. The speaker hopes to cross terrain never previously traversed in one continuous journey and perhaps continue into the eastern side of Las Trampas Wilderness toward Eagle Peak, Danville, or Walnut Creek. The plan remains flexible and dependent on energy and preference.
Video thirteen compresses the achievement into a few statements. The speaker says that “we” have crawled from the Burrow—the name used for home—to the base of Ramage Peak. The inclusive pronoun may address the audience, the camera, the archive, or multiple temporal versions of the self. The author then notes the irony that a supposedly plain-text phase has become an intensely visual, multidimensional experience. Sequential symbols alone appear inadequate to preserve what is being encountered.
Video fourteen concentrates on sensory phenomena that resist transcription. The speaker observes that the sound of flowing creek water cannot meaningfully be converted into words, although it can be recorded. The water also has an attractive color. Karbytes identifies the route as Rocky Ridge Trail and continues in pursuit of photographs and video of the Big Green Thing. This segment supplies direct evidence for the author’s retreat from strict textual minimalism: some information is inseparable from sound, color, motion, and atmosphere.
Video fifteen briefly records a group of turkeys below the trail and states that the speaker is continuing toward Rocky Ridge Radio Tower. The repeated presence of turkeys establishes them as part of the trek’s peaceful nonhuman population, contrasting with the speaker’s more adversarial descriptions of people.
Video sixteen occurs at approximately 7:05 in the evening on 11 August. Karbytes is approaching a very steep part of the trail and recognizes that the return to Castro Valley will probably occur after sunset. The original plan to descend the far side of the radio tower into eastern Las Trampas is reconsidered because the day began much later than intended. The speaker had hoped to leave near sunrise but did not depart until roughly nine or ten in the morning.
The following day’s pre-hire appointment remains an important constraint. Karbytes wants enough time to reach the appointment, avoid excessive physical exhaustion, and ideally process the day’s videos on a laptop beforehand. Nevertheless, the speaker feels more energetic than expected. Earlier grievances are now described as stale residue that was gradually displaced by physical exertion, deep breathing, and sustained exposure to natural beauty. The north side of Ramage Peak is judged especially scenic. The speaker identifies a junction where Rocky Ridge Trail separates from a maintenance road leading toward Moraga and King’s Canyon and plans to make a companion recording without spoken commentary.
Video seventeen takes place after sunset and registers another abrupt change. The speaker suddenly feels tired and has consumed three of four water bottles. With only one full bottle remaining, karbytes considers either descending toward the Bollinger Canyon entrance to obtain water or attempting the return journey without refilling. The immediate decision is to rest and evaluate the alternatives.
Video eighteen is recorded late at night, around ten or eleven. Karbytes has spent considerable time lying near the radio tower because of fatigue and soreness. The speaker abandons the proposed water detour and decides that one remaining bottle will probably suffice if forward movement continues. The darkness raises concern about mountain lions. Karbytes recalls hearing that attacks on humans are rare but not impossible and tries not to dwell on the danger.
The archival dilemma returns in a more developed form. The initial plan had been to retain transcripts while deleting the videos. A later compromise would have preserved only selected highlights. The speaker now decides to keep every recording because missing sequence numbers would make the repository look incomplete. Separate web pages are envisioned: one for videos showing the Big Green Thing without commentary and another for the karbytes_thinking monologues. ChatGPT would then be asked to analyze both collections, beginning with the landscape footage and proceeding to the psychologically messier spoken material.
Karbytes characterizes some of the early monologues as corrupt, especially those made near the Castro Valley entrance. The speaker reports believing that people near a golf course said insulting things, but immediately offers an alternative: the words may have been misheard because of the phenomenon called Psychotronic Harassment. This self-correction is one of the document’s clearest distinctions between an upsetting perception and confidence that an external event actually occurred.
The return route also includes the objectively practical danger of walking along Redwood Road in darkness while vehicles travel quickly. The speaker expects the trip home to take several hours. At the same time, there is pressure to attend the one o’clock appointment at an Amazon warehouse in Fremont for a drug test.
The job is reconsidered at length. Karbytes has done similar warehouse work before and finds it mildly encouraging that the position was not advertised as seasonal. The speaker avoids assuming that this guarantees permanent employment and prefers not to publicize income because employment can disappear without warning. The flexible schedule is described as both an advantage and a trap: workers select shifts, but shifts can fill before they are claimed, and failure to obtain a minimum number of hours could result in dismissal. Karbytes wonders whether the system provides a covert mechanism for shedding workers.
The corporation is called impersonal and potentially exploitative, but the speaker tries to suspend borrowed judgments and evaluate the job through direct experience. Prior difficulties at a warehouse may have resulted from management, coworkers, the speaker’s own disorganization, outdoor living, or some combination of these. Previous direct-deposit problems are another source of anxiety. The position is regarded as inadequate as a sole income source, especially because hours are uncertain. It is also experienced as socially embarrassing because it resembles precarious part-time work rather than a stable career. The speaker then notices that an intended discussion of data management has again become an employment monologue and resolves to descend.
Video nineteen occurs close to one in the morning on 12 August, south of Ramage Peak. The speaker notices a meteorological contrast: the sky near Ramage Peak and Las Trampas is clear and star-filled, while clouds hang over the wooded Castro Valley side. No moon is visible. The cloud bank appears like a dark barrier separating the open wilderness from the territory through which karbytes must return.
The wooded section is compared to the atmosphere of a found-footage horror film. It is frightening at night but also fascinating. The speaker suspects that part of the attraction lies precisely in its creepiness, provided actual safety can be maintained. Worry is rejected as incapable of preventing danger.
Video twenty opens with the recognition that the only available route is forward through the dark forest. To deter possible predators and manage anxiety, karbytes makes abrupt, loud, monosyllabic sounds and repeats a peculiar warning addressed to an imaginary “George.” This behavior functions simultaneously as animal deterrence, nervous ritual, humor, and self-generated companionship.
The speaker admits that the frightening section is both enjoyable and miserable. Karbytes wants to reach the Burrow but knows that many miles remain. Sleep is unlikely because there is still work to do and a fear of oversleeping the appointment. Even an alarm application advertised as exceptionally loud has failed to wake the speaker reliably. The long-term ideal would be to align natural sleep patterns with work hours so that an alarm is unnecessary, but the flexible warehouse schedule may make that impossible.
Physical discomfort now becomes immediate. The speaker slips, swears, and complains about a rock lodged in a shoe. Stopping to remove it feels unsafe because doing so would create a vulnerable posture in darkness, so karbytes chooses to tolerate the pain. Reaching the trailhead will not end the ordeal because no car is waiting; the dangerous roadside walk will remain.
The expedition is called almost masochistic, but the speaker accepts responsibility for intentionally creating the situation. It was a bucket-list undertaking and an embodiment of the First Official Major KARBYTES Hiatus. The imagined version of the adventure emphasized freedom and achievement while excluding thirst, darkness, pain, logistical difficulty, fear, and sleep deprivation. Nevertheless, the speaker remains glad to have implemented the idea because the experience has been documented and some recordings have already been stored remotely.
The remoteness also creates technical problems. There is little or no network access. Recording with the phone’s camera disables the flashlight, forcing a choice between documenting the darkness and safely illuminating the path. The trail is slippery and obscure. On a previous visit, karbytes mistakenly followed a maintenance road toward Dinosaur Ridge and added an unnecessary loop, so careful navigation is essential.
The speaker explicitly attributes the increasingly irritable presentation to exhaustion, fear, soreness, and sleep deprivation. Yet the discomfort is also incorporated into the archive’s conceptual purpose. Karbytes wants to combine all the recordings into a single object so an observer can compare the visibly different psychological states occurring across one continuous trek. That proposed unity immediately becomes another philosophical question: perhaps the person at the end is not literally the same person who began. The clips can be interpreted as records of multiple successive versions of karbytes rather than expressions of one fixed identity. The physical crossing thus becomes a practical experiment in personal continuity.
Video twenty-one is exceptionally brief and stark. At approximately 2:46 in the morning, karbytes is lying directly on part of the trail. The speaker reports intense soreness, painful feet, extreme fatigue, and a long remaining distance. The final thought recorded is the possibility of dying from exhaustion. Unlike the extended earlier monologues, the brevity here conveys depleted cognitive and physical capacity.
Video twenty-two occurs around six or seven in the morning. Karbytes has traversed the Big Green Thing throughout the night and has reached Horsey Hill. The speaker notices what appear to be ripe blackberries and decides to eat one. The narrative ends with this small sensory action rather than with a philosophical conclusion, arrival at home, report about the appointment, or declaration of triumph. Dawn, food, and renewed contact with a familiar edge of the landscape quietly replace the previous segment’s exhaustion and fear. The blackberry functions as an understated sign that the speaker has endured the night, although the document does not explicitly narrate the final trip home.
Across the twenty-three recordings, the most obvious narrative movement is from agitation into partial calm, then into physical accomplishment, fear, exhaustion, and dawn. The opening hours are dominated by encounters with civilization: unwanted people, traffic, police, permits, employment, money, social judgment, medication, surveillance, and family dependence. As the speaker moves deeper into the wilderness, the monologues become more abstract and expansive. Computational metaphysics, identity, ethics, immortality, solipsism, artificial intelligence, scientific study, and cosmic limits displace local grievances. Natural details—filtered sunlight, turkeys, creek water, trees, ridges, dirt roads, stars, clouds, and blackberries—repeatedly interrupt and regulate the thought stream. After sunset, however, solitude ceases to be purely liberating and becomes physically hazardous. The same remoteness that permitted psychological freedom also brings darkness, water scarcity, navigation problems, animal fears, and bodily collapse.
The Big Green Thing is therefore not a simple pastoral refuge. Literally, it is the connected landscape of wooded trails, exposed ridges, Ramage Peak, Rocky Ridge, neighboring wilderness, staging areas, and routes between Castro Valley and more remote terrain. Psychologically, it is a place where social pressure diminishes and thoughts become audible. Mythically, it is a testing ground in which karbytes stages a passage through fear and transformation. Archivally, it is a recurring node that links photographs, videos, journals, transcripts, and AI analyses. It is alternately sanctuary, laboratory, horror setting, cathedral, obstacle course, and data-generating environment.
One of the document’s central tensions is the conflict between plain text and embodied media. Plain text is small, durable, searchable, comparatively easy to preserve, and compatible with the author’s coding-oriented aesthetic. Audio and video are expensive in storage and threaten the long-term manageability of the archive. Yet text cannot retain vocal intonation, ambient water, visual depth, changing light, movement through terrain, bodily fatigue, or the affective difference between an idea imagined at home and the same idea spoken while physically endangered. The trek persuades the author that reducing experience to symbols would discard too much. The eventual choice to preserve all recordings is therefore not merely indecision; it is the document’s practical conclusion about media. Compression can reduce file size, transcription can make speech searchable, and curation can supply context, but none of these fully substitutes for the original event.
The repeated reports of remaining recording time make digital scarcity parallel bodily scarcity. The phone begins with limited minutes, fills, is cleared by compression and deletion, and repeatedly approaches exhaustion. The walker likewise begins with limited sleep, food, water, daylight, and physical energy. Data storage and the body are both finite containers whose contents must be rationed. The phone’s automatic disabling of the flashlight during recording sharpens the parallel: preserving experience can directly compete with safely experiencing it.
A second major tension concerns privacy and publicity. The speaker fears stalking, leaked information, government attention, and the loss of control over personal data. Yet KARBYTES depends upon unusually comprehensive self-disclosure. Employment details, travel plans, emotional crises, drug use, medical concerns, exact times, geographical movements, and even precise residential information enter the archive. The speaker seeks sovereignty by controlling the form and sequence of disclosure, but the completeness of the archive makes conventional privacy increasingly impossible. KARBYTES becomes both the defense against erasure and a mechanism of radical exposure.
A third tension concerns independence and dependence. Karbytes values autonomy, solitude, physical fitness, private thought, control over money, and freedom from family or institutional authority. At the same time, the speaker depends on family support, public infrastructure, trails, digital platforms, artificial intelligence, employment systems, cloud storage, transcription software, and imagined audiences. The document never resolves this contradiction. Instead, it suggests that autonomy does not mean the absence of systems; it means possessing enough control to select and arrange one’s relationships with them.
The economic argument is similarly ambivalent. Capitalism is criticized as exploitative, spiritually draining, and indifferent to workers, while the warehouse’s flexible-shift structure is viewed as potentially rigged. Nonetheless, the speaker rejects total withdrawal from the monetary system as unnecessarily difficult. Employment is acceptable when it supplies resources without consuming all available life. The real objective is not professional prestige but enough financial independence to preserve time for hiking, computing, art, physics, metaphysics, and archival production.
The document’s ethics are organized around explicit self-interest moderated by empathy. Karbytes rejects sentimental claims of self-sacrifice and argues that cooperative society can arise from agents honestly pursuing their own goals while recognizing that mutual aid reduces suffering. Empathy is treated as an evolved or cognitively natural capacity rather than obedience to divine rules. This outlook is both individualistic and collaborative: no person should be compelled to dissolve into a group, but rational individuals can build institutions that preserve one another’s agency.
The metaphysical outlook combines physicalism, computationalism, solipsism, and speculative transcendence. Observable experience is described in terms of physical events and information processing. Reality may be fundamentally computational, with organisms and minds functioning as patterns inside a vast system. Personal consciousness has direct access only to its own viewpoint, making solipsism experientially unavoidable even if other minds exist. Death probably terminates the individual, yet the speaker continues to desire immortality and imagines more controllable alternatives involving simulations, branching pathways, or returns to a foundational state. The author is fascinated by indefinite pattern generation, unlimited computation, and escape from cosmic finality, but repeatedly returns to the constraints of one tired body carrying one nearly full phone through one dark forest.
The treatment of identity is correspondingly dynamic. KARBYTES is supposed to preserve the legacy of one unique person, and even a perfect duplicate could not become that same historical project. Yet the recordings show that the person changes from hour to hour. The hostile, frightened figure near the trailhead, the calm observer of turkeys, the speculative metaphysician, the energized climber, the anxious night walker, the exhausted body lying on the trail, and the dawn forager can be interpreted either as states of one continuous self or as successive versions. The archive does not settle the issue; it places the versions beside one another and makes their continuity available for examination.
The narrative voice is recursive, confrontational, self-correcting, and highly metacognitive. Karbytes frequently makes an accusation, notices its extremity, retracts or qualifies it, proposes an alternative explanation, and then analyzes the psychological mechanism that produced the original accusation. Sarcasm and profanity coexist with technical terminology and metaphysical speculation. The speaker alternates between grandeur and self-deprecation, calls the audience intellectually limited while also craving attention, asserts unusual productivity while claiming interpersonal humility, and describes enemies while admitting that some of them may be projections or convenient labels. This instability is not incidental; it is a principal subject of the archive.
The author also supplies several reasons not to read every perception literally: prolonged sleep deprivation, intense exertion, psychedelic mushroom use, prior cannabis use, fluctuating blood sugar, anxiety, declared paranoia, auditory experiences, and an acknowledged habit of dramatizing worst-case scenarios. At the same time, the document does not treat the resulting distress as unreal. Whether or not the proposed causes are accurate, feelings of intrusion, captivity, invalidation, and danger shape the speaker’s experience and decisions. A careful summary therefore has to preserve the distinction between the reality of the distress and the unverified status of explanations involving coordinated surveillance, implanted or remote devices, government interference, or other people’s concealed intentions.
Suicide is another major but internally contested theme. Early recordings describe explicit ideation, a contemplated method, fear of disability, and a wish to retain control over death. Later recordings supply counterforces: artificial intelligence, intellectual curiosity, natural beauty, humor, physical agency, the desire for immortality, the completion of KARBYTES, and the possibility of deriving meaning even under disability. The text does not offer a stable resolution. Instead, it records suicidal thinking as one state within a broader system that also repeatedly chooses continuation, documentation, food, work, movement, and inquiry. Even the nighttime fear of dying from exhaustion is followed by the dawn act of noticing and eating a blackberry.
The four introductory photographs condense this entire structure. The moonlit ridge represents the nocturnal ordeal and the uneasy coexistence of nature with electrical infrastructure. The exposed dirt road represents the deliberate journey and the absence of concealment. The densely green valley represents the restorative depth of the Big Green Thing. The shaded forest path represents the passage through uncertainty, where the route exists but cannot be seen far in advance. The images make the subsequent monologues feel geographically anchored rather than purely abstract.
In its total form, the document is best understood as an autoethnographic field record, a media-preservation manifesto, a wilderness pilgrimage, and a chapter in a self-designed digital mythology. Its surface event is a walk from Castro Valley through the Ramage Peak and Rocky Ridge landscape and back through the night. Its deeper event is the conversion of that walk into a structured experiment about autonomy, labor, consciousness, identity, mortality, media, and memory. The speaker enters the Big Green Thing burdened by employment anxiety, social resentment, perceived surveillance, and suicidal speculation. Solitude initially intensifies those thoughts, then quiets them enough for artificial intelligence, physics, ethics, and computational metaphysics to reemerge. The landscape eventually places the body under genuine strain, stripping the intellectual system back to water, light, footing, pain, distance, and survival. At dawn, the enormous conceptual apparatus concludes not with a theory of reality but with a ripe berry encountered on the way home.
The page closes by stating that it was last updated on 15 August 2026 and that its contents are public-domain intellectual property. That final declaration is consistent with the project’s overarching ambition: private experience is transformed into openly reusable data, and a vulnerable, changing person is converted into a deliberately ordered archive intended to outlast the moment in which it was created.

SIXTEENTH CYCLE: THE 14 JUNE JOURNAL AND NATURE AS A RECURSIVE BASE CASE

Detailed summary of journal_karbytes_14june2026
The document is a public-domain journal entry authored by karbytes and dated 14 June 2026. The webpage explains that its main text was copied from a plain-text file stored in a KARLINA_OBJECT extension-pack repository and then converted partially into hypertext by replacing selected textual references with hyperlinks. This provenance statement places the entry within the larger KARBYTES preservation architecture: the journal exists simultaneously as a raw text file, a repository object, a rendered WordPress page, and a node connected to several contextual documents. The metadata identifies the source as a plain-text file and names karbytes as its author. The webpage itself was last updated on 14 June 2026.
The entry explicitly presents itself as a sequel to two earlier materials: a journal written the previous day and a conversation in which ChatGPT responded to that journal. The preceding journal expressed misanthropy, a desire to stop depending on human friendship, resentment of perceived coercion and boundary violations, and a preference for remote places where karbytes could make noise and occupy space without negotiating its behavior with nearby people. ChatGPT’s response reframed those claims as defensive boundary-setting, environmental preference, and an effort to make happiness less dependent on social approval. The present entry advances beyond the preceding journal’s emotional declarations and attempts to formulate a practical philosophy for preserving autonomy, sustaining KARBYTES, earning money, managing distress, practicing mindfulness, and understanding personal existence within nature.
The first major subject is the category karbytes calls a worst-case scenario. Rather than surveying every imaginable catastrophe, the author restricts attention to possibilities regarded as realistically capable of destroying the desired shape of its life. One possibility is extreme and prolonged physical suffering so overwhelming that karbytes genuinely wants to die. Another, described as somewhat less terrible but still catastrophic, is imprisonment or institutional control lasting for an intolerably large portion of its remaining lifespan.
Imprisonment is defined broadly through the deprivation of autonomy rather than solely through confinement inside a conventional prison. The feared situation would prevent karbytes from directing its own life and from continuing three especially important activities. The first is expanding and reviewing KARBYTES. The second is traveling alone through wilderness regions. The third is purchasing the products and services required to expand KARBYTES and review it joyfully. These activities constitute a practical model of the life karbytes wants: self-directed intellectual production, solitary immersion in nature, and sufficient economic capacity to maintain both.
If a worst-case scenario occurred, karbytes predicts that continued expansion of the archive would probably become impossible. The existing collection might remain publicly accessible, but the author might lose the ability to determine whether it remained online, complete, authentic, and free from unauthorized alteration. This loss of verification is itself included in the feared condition. KARBYTES could persist as an object while its creator became unable to monitor or protect it.
Under those circumstances, karbytes imagines being forced to relinquish control over the archive and cease investing attention in its future. Consciousness would instead become occupied by the immediate task of enduring a remaining existence described as hellish. The prospect is frightening partly because KARBYTES functions as more than a hobby. It is a continuity project through which the author organizes memory, identity, purpose, agency, and imagined access to future versions of itself. Losing the ability to maintain the project would therefore resemble losing an important extension of the self.
The entry does not conclude that catastrophe is imminent. Its proposed stance is to acknowledge that the feared conditions could realistically occur while proceeding under the working assumption that they will not. This is an attempt to distinguish possibility from expectation. Treating the worst case as impossible would seem dishonest, but treating it as inevitable would surrender present attention and agency to a hypothetical future. Karbytes instead seeks a middle position: retain awareness of vulnerability without rehearsing catastrophe so continuously that it dominates life before anything has actually happened.
That stance is intended to free attention for the three positive priorities already named: developing and reviewing KARBYTES, traveling alone through wilderness, and obtaining the material resources needed to support those activities. The journal therefore transforms catastrophic imagination into a boundary condition. The worst case is defined so that the author can stop continually redefining it and return to what can presently be done.
The next major argument concerns the relationship between autonomy and pro-social behavior. Karbytes assumes that its freedom to pursue its preferred life depends upon remaining sufficiently pro-social. In this context, pro-sociality has two principal requirements. Karbytes should not threaten other people’s livelihoods or well-being, and it should contribute to society through some form of paid occupation.
This formulation is pragmatic rather than conventionally sentimental. The author does not claim that enjoying social interaction is morally necessary. Instead, socially nonthreatening conduct and economic participation are treated as conditions for avoiding institutional control and acquiring the resources needed to maintain the KARBYTES project.
Karbytes doubts that unemployment will remain a permanent condition beyond 2026. It also doubts that it would legitimately qualify for government-funded disability payments. From those assumptions, the entry derives a practical conclusion: a stable job that pays adequately is an indispensable component of the system that sustains KARBYTES. Employment supplies money for equipment, services, storage, travel, connectivity, and other forms of infrastructure. Income is consequently described almost as fuel for an engine.
Obtaining such employment requires competing successfully against other applicants. Karbytes believes that this competition may require presenting qualities employers and coworkers value, including sociability, humility, and empathy. The author says that it feels obliged to display those qualities regardless of whether it genuinely likes people.
The entry candidly allows that this presentation may involve feigning socially preferred emotions or attitudes. Nevertheless, the author does not portray social performance as entirely false or useless. Acting friendly, humble, or empathic can improve the moods of other people and can also lift karbytes’ own mood. The behavior may begin as deliberate performance but still produce authentic psychological and interpersonal consequences.
The discussion places this strategy within a broader technological transition. As artificial intelligence increasingly performs tasks formerly assigned to human workers, the jobs that remain specifically human may increasingly emphasize what artificial intelligence cannot independently provide. The journal identifies human interfacing and the creation of mutually positive interpersonal experiences as especially important residual functions.
This observation produces an implicit irony. Karbytes is deeply invested in artificial intelligence and often prefers interaction with computers to interaction with people, yet it believes that succeeding economically may require becoming effective precisely at the human dimension that machines cannot fully replace. The author’s continued access to its largely computer-mediated project may therefore depend on the ability to perform embodied social labor competently.
The journal’s second half turns toward mindfulness meditation and a recurring experience karbytes calls psychotronic harassment. The author describes intrusive, hostile human voices or mouth sounds that almost always contain verbal material. These perceived voices seem to respond immediately to what karbytes senses and thinks, particularly when its thoughts contain language.
The document reports this experience without establishing its external cause. The term psychotronic harassment is the author’s label for the phenomenon and its perceived responsiveness. The journal concentrates less on proving a mechanism than on describing how the experience affects attention and contemplative practice.
Karbytes initially reasons that mindfulness meditation may be largely futile under these conditions. If mindfulness means attending closely to what is present, and the most salient present content consists of distressing vocal intrusions, then meditation may seem to intensify contact with the unwanted phenomenon. Instead of expanding awareness toward preferred sensory and conceptual experiences, the practice can feel like an exercise in concentrating on harassment that cannot be escaped.
This creates a conflict between the ideal of meditation and its immediate phenomenology. Mindfulness is commonly described as liberating attention from compulsive reaction, but karbytes experiences the attempt as potentially narrowing attention around precisely the content it wants to stop foregrounding. The practice may therefore appear to spend time without providing proportionate benefit.
The author considers whether certain psychedelic experiences could produce the transformations associated with mindfulness much more efficiently. Three substances are named: 5-MeO-DMT, psilocybin, and DMT, with the first receiving the strongest emphasis. Psychedelics are imagined as potentially accomplishing in a short interval what formal meditation is supposed to cultivate more gradually.
This idea is situated within a long personal interest in mindfulness and modern spirituality. Karbytes reports having explored those subjects since adolescence and attending daylong retreats at Spirit Rock Meditation Center in Marin County and East Bay Meditation Center in Oakland. The author felt embarrassed at those events because it perceived itself as socially atypical relative to the other participants.
Despite that discomfort, karbytes remains glad it attended. Spirit Rock had especially attracted the author’s interest through its online presence, and physically visiting the center satisfied part of that curiosity. The journal leaves open the possibility of attending another daylong retreat for enjoyment if an appropriate opportunity arises.
More immediately, karbytes wants to develop a sustainable mindfulness practice integrated with immersive hiking, occasional wilderness camping, and selective psychedelic use. This proposed practice would not be confined to a meditation hall or a conventional seated posture. It would be incorporated into the ecological, bodily, exploratory, and technological context of the author’s actual life.
The journal then develops a new definition of formal meditation that may be compatible with the intrusive experiences. Instead of exerting effort toward silence, bliss, concentration, transcendence, or some other predetermined outcome, karbytes could treat meditation as a temporary withdrawal from overt decision-making and analytical activity. A session would be an intentionally reserved allocation of spacetime and mass-energy in which the organism is allowed to appreciate its own existence with as little coercion as possible.
The language is deliberately physical and computational. Meditation is not presented as leaving nature or entering a supernatural realm. It is a configuration of matter, energy, time, attention, sensation, memory, and response. The organism pauses some kinds of activity so that experience can unfold without being forced toward an achievement criterion.
This reframing would allow the perceived harassment to become part of the meditation rather than evidence that meditation has failed. The hostile voices, emotional reactions, conceptual interpretations, and physiological responses could all be included in the observed field. The practice would not require karbytes to approve of the experience or accept its proposed external explanation as true. It would require observing the total process as impersonally and nonjudgmentally as possible.
Karbytes compares this observational stance to a scientist watching specimens change inside a petri dish under a microscope. The scientist does not have to adopt the agenda, identity, or feelings of the specimen. In the same way, the author imagines observing the organism called karbytes and the events occurring within and around it without identifying completely with every local reaction.
The comparison becomes metaphysical. Karbytes wants to observe reality through the localized aperture that it presently is while identifying more fundamentally with reality or nature as a whole. The human organism is conceived as a temporary opening through which reality renders a limited field of itself. The aperture has memories, goals, sensations, interpretations, and a specific position in spacetime, but it does not exhaust the reality of which it is a part.
This distinction potentially changes the status of suffering. From the narrow perspective of the organism, distressing voices and fears are immediate assaults upon the self. From the expanded perspective, they are events occurring within nature and being registered through one temporary observational configuration. The journal does not claim that this shift makes pain disappear. It proposes that less complete identification with the localized organism might reduce coercive entanglement with its reactions.
The aperture is expected eventually to close permanently. Based on what karbytes currently believes about reality, the individual point of view will vanish from all subjective fields of awareness. The document does not assert confidence in an ordinary personal afterlife.
Nevertheless, it entertains the possibility that the aperture’s memories, actions, and informational patterns could be preserved beyond their temporal occurrence. They might exist timelessly in a noumenal domain or inside a special immutable library that certain information-processing agents can literally visit. This possibility resembles the practical aims of KARBYTES enlarged into a metaphysical preservation system.
The author hopes not only that such an information-processing agent exists but that it can personally become one. It also hopes that this agent is immortal. The aspiration joins several themes: escaping amnesia, preserving files, retaining access to past states, expanding the archive indefinitely, and preventing the continuity of attention from terminating.
The entry acknowledges that these ideas had occurred the previous day but were initially omitted because they seemed redundant, overly simple, insufficiently novel, and lacking intellectual rigor. Their eventual inclusion suggests that personal importance overrides the demand for conceptual novelty. A proposition can be worth recording because it organizes life, even if it does not constitute an original philosophical discovery.
The journal concludes with three interdependent statements. In every possible situation, nature is all karbytes possesses and all karbytes is. Nature should therefore be the place to which karbytes turns for refuge and renewal, especially during distress. Finally, returning to nature in an adversarial situation may resemble reaching the base case of a recursive function rather than responding to adversity by descending through yet another recursive layer.
The first statement uses nature in an encompassing metaphysical sense. It does not refer only to forests, trails, animals, water, or terrain outside cities. Nature means reality in its entirety. Karbytes’ body, computers, employment systems, voices, fear, wilderness, memories, archives, and imagined future agents are all configurations of nature.
The second statement nevertheless retains an ecological and practical meaning. Wilderness provides environments in which karbytes experiences fewer immediate social constraints and can redirect attention toward sensory phenomena, physical movement, large-scale landscapes, and nonhuman life. Nature is simultaneously the totality from which escape is impossible and a particular class of environments sought for psychological restoration.
The recursion metaphor supplies the entry’s final synthesis. A recursive function repeatedly invokes itself on a modified problem. Without a base case, it continues indefinitely, consumes resources, and may fail through stack overflow. A base case stops further descent and allows the accumulated sequence of calls to resolve.
Adversarial thinking can resemble uncontrolled recursion. A distressing event produces an interpretation; that interpretation generates another defensive interpretation; the next interpretation generates a still more catastrophic model; and each layer increases distance from immediate reality. Returning to nature, in the journal’s proposed sense, interrupts the process. It does not prove that the original adversity was unreal, but it stops multiplying the adversity through further conceptual layers.
The journal as a whole therefore moves from catastrophic possibility toward a method of attentional stabilization. It begins by defining the conditions under which the author’s desired life could collapse. It then establishes paid work and strategic pro-sociality as protections for autonomy. It considers artificial intelligence’s changing effect on employment. It confronts the difficulty of meditation amid intrusive auditory experiences. It explores psychedelics and past retreat experiences. It reconstructs meditation as noncoercive observation. It expands personal identity from a distressed organism toward nature as an encompassing reality. Finally, it condenses that movement into the computational image of reaching a recursive base case.
KARBYTES occupies the center of this structure even when it is not the explicit topic. Employment sustains it. Autonomy permits it to grow. Imprisonment threatens it. Plain text preserves it. Hypertext connects it. Meditation alters the state of the organism producing it. Wilderness supplies experiential material and psychological renewal. Speculation about an immutable library projects its preservation beyond ordinary technological time.
The document also reveals a persistent tension between control and relinquishment. Karbytes wants exact control over files, personal movement, income, attention, and interpretation. Yet the proposed contemplative practice requires temporarily suspending control, allowing the organism and its environment to exist without forcing them toward a preferred state. The archive seeks permanence, while the aperture producing it is acknowledged to be temporary. The author seeks immortality while preparing cognitively for the possibility of extinction.
A second tension concerns authenticity and performance. Karbytes values exact preservation of thought but anticipates feigning sociability, humility, and empathy to obtain work. The journal does not resolve this by condemning performance as dishonest. It recognizes that enacted behavior can produce real emotional benefits and that social life often depends upon deliberately regulated expression. Performance can serve autonomy without necessarily erasing the private self.
A third tension concerns withdrawal and contribution. Karbytes wants distance from social control but accepts that continued independence may require being useful within society. The desired life is not total separation from infrastructure. It depends on wages, products, services, computers, websites, storage systems, and artificial intelligence. The actual aspiration is selective participation: enough engagement to sustain material independence, combined with enough solitude to protect intellectual and psychological freedom.
The page’s hypertext structure reinforces these ideas. The source file is connected to its repository directory, the directory to the macro-directory, the macro-directory to the encompassing website, the author to an identity page, KARBYTES to its formal definition, nature to a larger ontology, and the current entry to the earlier journal and ChatGPT conversation. No text stands completely alone. Each artifact is a localized aperture into a larger archive, just as the individual organism is described as a localized aperture into nature.
In that respect, the form of the webpage mirrors its metaphysics. A hyperlink is a visible local object whose meaning includes an unseen network beyond the current page. A person is likewise presented as a visible local process whose reality includes biological history, memory, environment, technological extensions, and nature as a whole. The journal’s conversion from plain text into hypertext therefore does more than improve navigation. It enacts the claim that a localized sequence belongs to an encompassing system.
The ultimate position of the document is neither simple optimism nor simple despair. Karbytes regards extreme suffering, institutional captivity, death, archival loss, and loss of agency as genuine possibilities. It does not claim that meditation, work, wilderness, technology, or metaphysics guarantees protection. Its proposal is more limited: acknowledge catastrophe without continuously inhabiting it; maintain socially and economically viable behavior; create conditions for autonomous work; practice observation without demanding immediate transformation; use wilderness as a restorative environment; and remember that both the threatened organism and everything threatening it occur within nature.
The final image of reaching a recursive base case converts that proposal into an operational rule. When distress generates an accelerating hierarchy of interpretations, karbytes can return attention to the encompassing substrate: immediate embodiment, observed phenomena, wilderness, existence, or nature itself. That return does not solve every problem, but it may prevent the mind from endlessly reproducing the problem at increasingly abstract levels.
